#5084. Fed and OCC Quiz U.S. Banks After $6.3 Billion Binance Hack

Federal Reserve and OCC supervisors contacted U.S. banks this week to check for vulnerabilities after the Binance hack.

U.S. bank regulators contacted banks this week to check whether their systems had any vulnerabilities after last week’s $6.3 billion hack of Binance, Reuters reported Thursday, citing several people familiar with the matter.

Supervisors from the Federal Reserve and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency began reaching out to banks on Monday morning, asking about any unusual activity or disruption in their services.

The outreach shows regulators are treating the largest crypto theft on record as a possible risk to traditional finance, not just a problem for crypto exchanges, at a time when links between banks and the crypto industry have tightened.

One source at a bank that regulators contacted told Reuters there had been no disruptions to its services. Employees there prepared talking points for customers but have since received few inquiries.

Banks have been studying the attack and running diagnostics on their own systems to make sure they could defend against something similar, two people familiar with the efforts said.

Within hours of the hack, a cybersecurity team at one of the largest U.S. banks reached out to counterparts at Binance. That team is writing an internal report that will lead to a review of the bank’s systems and possibly cybersecurity updates. It was not yet clear whether the bank would be vulnerable to the same tactics.

The biggest concern, one person said, is that artificial intelligence models have been evolving quickly, making it hard for banks to keep up with hackers. Banks are devoting a lot of resources to reviewing and patching their cybersecurity code.

Binance said it was not aware of conversations between banks and regulators or whether the hack had any impact on U.S. banks.

“We are in close contact with regulators all over the world, keeping them up to date on this security breach,” a Binance spokesperson said.

The concern about AI tracks with how the attack unfolded. Binance said the theft began with a fake job interview and ran for five months before any coins moved. The campaign combined a poisoned software download, a staged recruitment process, malware that evaded endpoint detection and an internal AI tool that the attackers turned against the company’s own wallet engineers.

Funds began leaving Binance hot wallets across multiple blockchains on Oct. 29. Blockchain analysis firms Chainalysis and TRM Labs linked the hack to TraderTraitor, a unit associated with North Korea.

Binance reopened withdrawals in stages four days later, starting with bitcoin. It has said it froze or intercepted about $2.85 billion of the stolen assets and replenished 98% of affected tokens from its own funds.

None of the techniques Binance described are unique to crypto. Social engineering, malicious downloads and compromised internal tools are risks every large financial institution faces, which helps explain why bank cybersecurity teams moved within hours.

The checks come after U.S. regulators spent the past year and a half clearing the way for banks to do more in crypto. The OCC rescinded its requirement that national banks get supervisory nonobjection before taking on crypto custody and stablecoin activities in March 2025. The Fed withdrew guidance the following month that had required state member banks to notify it before starting crypto activities.

As more banks custody bitcoin, issue stablecoins and serve exchanges, a breach at a major crypto platform becomes a question for bank supervisors too.

Traditional lenders are not immune from large thefts of their own. French bank Credit Mutuel Arkea confirmed a cyberattack on Wednesday that allowed fraudulent transfers from its own account, a theft an onchain investigator has put at more than $133 million. The bank has not confirmed that figure and said customer assets were not affected.

Neither the Fed nor the OCC has publicly announced any new guidance tied to the Binance hack.

#5082. BitGo launches institutional trading and financial solutions business led by former NYDIG executive

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Pete Janney will lead the new BitGo Global Markets division, following the firm's acquisition of NYDIG’s institutional and trading business in August.

Digital asset infrastructure firm BitGo Holdings BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) announced the launch of BitGo Global Markets on Wednesday, its new institutional trading and financial solutions business bringing together global liquidity, execution, financing, derivatives, and settlement services.

The division will be headed up by former NYDIG executive Pete Janney following BitGo's acquisition of NYDIG's institutional and trading business on Aug. 27, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

About 30 employees joined BitGo following the NYDIG acquisition, bringing experience in derivatives, financing, collateral servicing, and structured products. Janney initially came aboard as head of financial infrastructure and brings extensive experience in trading and capital markets.

BitGo has been offering institutional trading through BitGo Prime since 2020, with that business becoming part of Global Markets alongside the expanded derivatives, collateral management, and structured finance solutions from NYDIG.

"BitGo has built a strong foundation across custody, liquidity, trading, financing, and settlement," BitGo CEO Mike Belshe said in the statement. "Global Markets brings these capabilities together with deeper capital markets expertise, allowing us to support more complex institutional strategies and serve a broader range of our clients' financial needs. Pete understands how institutions trade and manage risk, and I'm excited to have him leading Global Markets.”

Under the new unit, BitGo's Global Liquidity Layer will provide access to spot trading and large over-the-counter transactions, drawing liquidity from exchanges, market makers, and other counterparties, the firm said. Clients can also borrow against their crypto holdings to fund trading positions, with BitGo keeping the collateral in qualified custody, monitoring margins, and handling collateral calls or liquidations when needed.

Additionally, BitGo Global Markets will offer options, swaps, and custom structured products for eligible counterparties to hedge exposure, express market views, and manage portfolio risk, the firm said.

Global Markets will also provide managed investment strategies, private funds, and research, while BitGo's Go Network will handle clearing and settlement.

"Clients who own digital assets need sophisticated solutions that go beyond buy and hold," Janney said. "I look forward to accelerating that work and growing the Global Markets franchise at BitGo."

BTGO was trading up 3.4% in pre-market trading on Wednesday, according to TradingView, following a 3.7% rise on Tuesday.

#5080. ChatGPT Is Getting a GIF Maker Powered by OpenAI's Unannounced Images 2.6 Model

ChatGPT Is Getting a GIF Maker Powered by OpenAI's Unannounced Images 2.6 Model

The internet may soon have an unlimited supply of AI-generated dancing cats, courtesy of ChatGPT.

OpenAI is rolling out a feature that lets users generate animated GIFs directly inside ChatGPT, according to The Verge. The feature reportedly comes powered by its new image-generation model called Images 2.6, which OpenAI has yet to formally introduce through its usual product announcements.

Users can describe an animation in plain English or upload an existing photo and ask ChatGPT to bring it to life. Once the GIF is generated, they can adjust its playback speed, inspect individual frames, make edits, and share the finished animation.

OpenAI demonstrated the capability with a simple request: “can you make a gif of a cat dancing?” The resulting interface reportedly includes controls to change animation speed and examine the GIF frame by frame.

We tried generating GIFs using ChatGPT, but the feature was not available on our accounts at the time of testing. In our interaction, the model generates a series of frames, but not the animation.

The rollout appears to be gradual, so users may have to keep asking for their dancing cats a little longer.

The GIF maker is reportedly part of a broader Images 2.6 update that promises faster image generation. The Verge reports that OpenAI is bringing the model to ChatGPT and Codex users across web, desktop, and mobile platforms.

Developers are also getting two new API models, GPT-Image-2.6 Flare and GPT-Image-2.6 Sunburst, designed for faster generation and higher-quality results, respectively. OpenAI has not yet published a standalone announcement detailing the new model's capabilities, and we could not independently test its performance.

The update follows the September release of ChatGPT Images 2.5, which introduced sharper details, more precise editing, and generation speeds up to 50% faster than its predecessor.

In our previous testing, Images 2.5 went head-to-head with Google's Nano Banana 2 across six categories, with each model winning three. OpenAI's model delivered particularly impressive illustration quality and improved on the oversharpening problems that affected earlier versions, although it still made mistakes when rendering text.

The move into GIF generation also comes months after OpenAI shut down Sora, its standalone AI video-generation app, in March. Animated GIFs are considerably simpler than the videos Sora generated, but the new feature brings a form of motion generation directly into ChatGPT without requiring a separate application.

OpenAI has not provided a specific date for when every ChatGPT account will receive GIF generation.

#5077. Ukraine Central Bank Refuses to Back Crypto Bill Cleared for Second Reading

Ukraine's National Bank says draft law 10225-d on virtual assets lacks safeguards on foreign tokens and platform licensing.

Ukraine’s central bank will not support a crypto market bill that a parliamentary committee cleared for a final vote, warning that the draft leaves foreign tokens that act like electronic money outside its control and risks monetary sovereignty.

The National Bank of Ukraine said in a Nov. 4 statement that Draft Law No. 10225-d on the circulation of virtual assets, recommended by the Verkhovna Rada Committee on Finance, Tax and Customs Policy for adoption in the second reading and as a whole, fails to reflect compromises struck during drafting.

The NBU said it received the final text only late the evening before the committee vote and that the process “was not sufficiently transparent.” Deputy Governor Oleksii Shaban raised the bank’s objections at the committee meeting, but the NBU said the format did not allow a full debate.

Ukraine already legalized a framework for virtual assets in 2022. The new bill is meant to put that market under detailed rules modeled on the European Union’s Markets in Crypto-Assets regulation, or MiCA, and on International Monetary Fund recommendations. The NBU says it supports that direction in principle.

The bank’s public case rests on three points.

First, the draft would let foreign tokens into Ukraine even when they function as electronic money but face no matching supervision in the countries where they are issued. That, the NBU said, threatens monetary sovereignty, the transmission of monetary policy, payment infrastructure and financial stability.

The bank wants the same power it has over domestically issued tokens: the authority to assess those foreign assets and, when needed, restrict their circulation in Ukraine.

Second, simplified admission of foreign virtual assets must not let them sidestep rules that apply to comparable Ukrainian assets. Without that parity, the NBU warned, segments of the market could expand outside adequate oversight and create regulatory arbitrage.

Third, the bank flagged monopolization and corruption risks in how the National Securities and Stock Market Commission would authorize trading-platform operators. The NBU initially opposed numerical caps, then agreed as a compromise to limit the first wave to three legal persons chosen by competitive selection.

The final draft, it said, drops a clear five-year cap and leaves the number of operators to the securities commission’s discretion without enough antitrust safeguards.

The committee’s Nov. 4 recommendation does not enact the law. A final Verkhovna Rada vote is expected later in November. If adopted, the rules would take effect on July 1, 2027, Interfax Ukraine reported, splitting supervision between the NBU and the securities commission.

Under the committee version, personal income from virtual-asset trades would face Ukraine’s standard personal income tax, with a reduced 5% rate through the end of 2028 for assets acquired before the law takes effect. Crypto-to-crypto exchanges would not be taxed, and virtual-asset deals would sit outside VAT.

The fight lands as Ukraine continues to debate how deep bitcoin and other digital assets should sit in state finance. Lawmakers last year introduced a separate bill that would let the NBU hold virtual assets, including bitcoin, in its foreign-exchange and gold reserves, a permission the bank would not be required to use.

“The development of the virtual asset market and the protection of monetary and financial stability are not mutually exclusive,” the NBU said. It called on members of parliament to refine the document with all parties to the process and said it remains ready to cooperate.

The test now is whether Ukraine’s lawmakers reopen the foreign-token and licensing clauses before a floor vote, or push the committee text through and leave the central bank on the outside of a framework it helped draft.

#5076. Circle reports $865 million in Q3 revenue and reserve income, discloses 5% workforce reduction

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Circle booked $182 million in ARC token presale revenue during the quarter, while income from its USDC reserves fell.

Stablecoin issuer Circle Internet Group Circle Internet Group, Inc. (CRCL) reported $865 million in total revenue and reserve income for the third quarter, up 17% from a year earlier, according to its earnings release on Wednesday.

Net income from continuing operations came in at $226 million, compared with $214.4 million in the same quarter last year. Adjusted EBITDA was $317 million, up from $171.5 million in Q3 2025. Circle previously reported $701 million in revenue and reserve income for the second quarter of this year, naming BlackRock, DTCC, and Visa among founding validators for its Arc blockchain.

Operating expenses rose 16% year over year to $245 million in the third quarter, the firm said, citing higher administrative, IT infrastructure, and marketing costs. Distribution, transaction, and other costs fell 8% to $412 million, mostly due to lower distribution payments, it added.

A footnote to the earnings tables also disclosed that Circle had reduced its workforce by approximately 5%, with the company booking $9.1 million in associated restructuring expenses, mainly for notice period and severance payments, employee benefits, and related costs. Circle said the reduction was designed to "better align our organizational structure with our operating model and strategic priorities," but did not give a headcount for the cuts. Circle had approximately 1,100 employees worldwide at the end of 2025, according to its annual report.

Of the $207 million Circle reported in other revenue, $182 million was recognized in Q3 from its ARC token presale. Other revenue had totaled $29 million in the third quarter of 2025. Circle agreed to sell a total of $242.2 million worth of ARC tokens in the presale at a purchase price of $0.30 per token and a $3 billion fully diluted valuation. Circle attributed the 85% increase in adjusted EBITDA to the presale revenue and lower distribution costs, partly offset by spending on new products.

Circle also maintained its full-year 2026 guidance, including $310 million to $330 million in other revenue and $570 million to $585 million in adjusted operating expenses.

Reserve income fell 8% year over year to $658 million as its reserve return rate declined by 61 basis points, the firm said. Circle's USDC stablecoin averaged $73.3 billion in circulation during Q3, an 8% increase from a year earlier. Onchain USDC transaction volume reached $13.9 trillion for the quarter, up 49% year over year. USDC circulation stood at $73.9 billion at quarter-end, unchanged from a year earlier, while Circle's stablecoin market share fell to 27%.

Circle opened Arc's public mainnet on Sept. 16, with more than 100 institutional and ecosystem builders on the network at launch. It also minted the initial supply of 10 billion ARC tokens, which the firm said was a technical milestone, not a commitment to launch them publicly. It expects Arc to move from proof of authority to proof of stake in 2027.

In September, Circle also agreed to acquire Tazapay, a Singapore-based payments firm with more than $25 billion in annualized payment volume. The firm expects the Tazapay acquisition to close in 2027, subject to regulatory approvals. Circle Ventures had led Tazapay's $36 million Series B extension in March. Binance also bought $100 million worth of Circle shares in September and signed a new five-year USDC agreement.

#5073. Why Bitcoin's Next Soft Fork Could Feel Like a Practice Run

Why Bitcoin's Next Soft Fork Could Feel Like a Practice Run

Four flaws in Bitcoin’s consensus rules that have lingered for years.

Issues like quantum resistance may command attention, but a practical cleanup is what many experts think Bitcoin’s next soft fork is likely to address, spanning fixes for vulnerabilities in the network’s difficulty adjustment mechanism to denial-of-service vectors among miners.

Proposed in 2019 and known as the Great Consensus Cleanup (BIP-54), the upgrade seeks to address the “cruft” in Bitcoin’s consensus design through changes that have historically been sidelined, as described by long-time Bitcoin developer and BIP-54 co-author Matt Corallo.

“There’s a laundry list of things we know of that we really need to clean up,” he said during a recent appearance on MARA Foundation TV. “All of these issues are real concerns that could impact Bitcoin negatively, but they’re not like, ‘Oh, the sky is falling.’”

Although a formal timeline for the BIP’s undertaking hasn’t been established, Corallo thinks there’s an 80% chance that the Great Consensus Cleanup is up next for the network. During his talk with Head of MARA Foundation Isabel Foxen Duke, he also called it a practical exercise for Bitcoin developers who haven’t yet been through a soft fork.

At its core, Corallo said BIP-54 “fixes some stuff that everyone agrees are problems,” while targeting four specific vulnerabilities:

Still, not everyone views a maintenance-focused soft fork as a major win. On X, Paul Sztorc, co-founder and CEO of LayerTwo Labs, called BIP-54’s potential activation “kind of depressing, since it is a janitorial maintenance fork that doesn't do anything.”

Surveying developers at TABconf in Atlanta, Sztorc noted that Bitcoin’s “best and brightest” predominantly expect the Great Consensus Cleanup to be Bitcoin’s next soft fork anyway, with the earliest estimate in September 2027, and most pointing to 2028 or later.

Following the activation of Taproot in 2021, Bitcoin’s community has faced years of gridlock surrounding protocol changes. This friction is frequently framed around “ossification,” with broader protocol changes like OP_CAT (BIP-347) and OP_CHECKTEMPLATEVERIFY (BIP-119) struggling to reach ecosystem-wide consensus.

According to Corallo, soft forks have always involved overcoming technical hurdles. But more recently, he’s observed political and social friction that’s become just as relevant.

The Bitcoin community spent months debating how Taproot should be activated, with arguments pitting miner-signaling thresholds against mandatory user flag days. Corallo argues that BIP-54 can establish a deployment playbook for future soft forks.

“In every recent soft fork, there’s also been a bunch of drama about activation mechanisms,” he said. “So hopefully we could do a soft fork to go through that drama, and then copy and paste and reuse it and head off some of that drama [for future soft forks].”

In that sense, BIP-54 is just as much about proving Bitcoin can still agree on upgrades at the social layer as it is about actual code cleanup.

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#5072. Bitcoin Price Crashes Over 5% to $75,000 as Tech Rout Wipes Out Leveraged Longs

Bitcoin price fell from above $80,800 to near $76,600 on Thursday morning as a Nasdaq selloff in chip and AI stocks spilled into crypto.

Bitcoin price fell about 5% on Thursday morning, sliding from above $80,800 to near $76,000 as a sharp selloff in technology stocks spread into crypto and wiped out leveraged traders betting on further gains, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows.

The drop erased the rebound that had carried bitcoin back above $80,000 overnight. It touched a low near $76,100 shortly after the U.S. stock market opened, its weakest level since the start of the week.

The reversal came fast. Bitcoin held near $80,000 through Wednesday, and early Thursday traders were cheering a short squeeze that pushed it past $80,500. Within roughly two hours of the opening bell, the bitcoin price was more than $4,000 lower.

The trigger sat on Wall Street. The Nasdaq Composite tumbled as much as 3.9% on Thursday and slipped into correction territory, trading 10% below the record close it set last month, CNBC reported. The S&P 500 fell about 2%, erasing Wednesday’s rally, its best session in three months.

Chip and AI stocks took the worst of it. Nvidia and Broadcom each fell about 5%, Tesla lost around 9% and neocloud names CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN dropped between 9% and 13%. Qualcomm slid 9% after a weak outlook. Sandisk was down nearly 19% at the time of writing.

Investors are pricing in a Democratic-controlled Congress after Tuesday’s midterms. Traders fear new limits on the data center buildout, and UBS analysts estimated that around 30% of U.S. data center capacity additions expected in 2028 could be at risk from construction pauses, Reuters reported. The selling started in Asia, where South Korea’s Kospi closed down more than 5% as Samsung and SK hynix sank.

U.S. stocks shed $2.3 trillion in market value within the first hour of trading, with the Russell 2000 plunging 2.5% and on pace for its biggest one-day drop since June 5

Bitcoin price has traded alongside that AI-heavy tech complex for most of the year, and bitcoin miners are now part of it. Many have sold bitcoin to fund AI data centers, so the same political risk now weighs on both.

Leverage turned the move into a flush. Roughly $370 million in leveraged crypto positions were liquidated in about an hour as bitcoin broke $77,000 and ether fell below $2,300, most of them long bets. Wider tallies circulating on X put long liquidations closer to $400 million over the same 60 minutes.

The U.S. stock market shed more than $1 trillion in value within minutes of the open, and crypto went with it.

Wednesday’s short squeeze had pulled in late buyers with borrowed money. When stocks broke, those positions were the first to go, and forced selling pushed the bitcoin price through support around $77,000.

The macro backdrop offered little cover. Initial jobless claims fell to 189,000 last week, matching July’s level, which was the lowest since 1969, Reuters reported. Economists had expected 196,000.

A labor market that tight gives the Federal Reserve little reason to ease. That leaves risk assets leaning on growth stories like AI to carry them, and that story is now under political pressure.

Fund flows were only a small offset. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs took in a net $74.6 million on Wednesday, breaking a three-day run of outflows, led by Fidelity’s FBTC while BlackRock’s IBIT saw money leave.

The next test is Friday’s October jobs report, followed by October CPI on Nov. 10 and the Fed’s Dec. 9 decision. Policy risk is building too, with House Democrats drafting their own market structure bill ahead of taking over the committees in January.

Traders are watching whether bitcoin can hold the $75,000 area, which held as support on election night and several times since August. A break would leave the bitcoin price near its lowest levels in months.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $76,200.

#5067. Arkansas Sues Kalshi, Joining a Growing List of States Targeting Prediction Markets

Arkansas Sues Kalshi, Joining a Growing List of States Targeting Prediction Markets

Arkansas has sued Kalshi, accusing the prediction market of offering an illegal, unregulated gambling platform to the state's residents, Attorney General Tim Griffin said on Wednesday.

The lawsuit, filed against KalshiEX LLC and Kalshi Inc., alleges multiple violations of the Arkansas Deceptive Trade Practices Act. The state is seeking restitution, penalties, taxes and an injunction.

Today I filed a lawsuit against @Kalshi for violating the Arkansas Deceptive Trade Practices Act by operating an unregulated gambling platform.

Kalshi has built a global business that it calls a prediction market, allowing consumers to wager money on the outcome of just about… pic.twitter.com/ePycNKXzy6

— Attorney General Tim Griffin (@AGTimGriffin) November 4, 2026

"It doesn't take a genius to understand that wagering money on whether the Razorbacks win or lose is gambling," Griffin said. "And gambling on an unregulated platform is squarely prohibited by Arkansas law."

Instead of obtaining licenses and paying the taxes required of gambling operators, Kalshi opened its platform to Arkansas residents as young as 18 and encouraged them to bet on sporting and entertainment events, Griffin alleged. "Arkansas law is concerned with substance, not semantics," he said.

Griffin pointed to Kalshi's own responsible-gambling tools, including self-exclusion, deposit limits and trading breaks, along with its partnership with the National Council on Problem Gambling, as evidence that the company knows its platform carries gambling-style risks. "Although Kalshi insists that its products are not gambling, its actions speak louder than its labels," he said.

Kalshi, which is regulated by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, has argued in other cases that its event contracts fall under exclusive federal jurisdiction and are not subject to state gambling laws.

Arkansas is the latest of more than a dozen states to take on prediction markets. Among the most significant cases:

Other states have also moved against prediction markets. Arizona filed criminal charges against Kalshi in March, a Washington court temporarily blocked its sports contracts in July, and Massachusetts' case is now before the state's highest court after a lower court barred Kalshi's sports contracts in January.

The CFTC has sued at least 10 states to defend what it says is its exclusive authority over prediction markets, and President Donald Trump has backed the agency, calling several state officials who challenged the platforms "scum."

Federal appeals courts are split. The Third Circuit ruled in April that New Jersey cannot regulate Kalshi's sports contracts, while the Sixth and Ninth Circuits sided with Ohio, Tennessee and Nevada. New Jersey has asked the Supreme Court to settle the question, backed by 39 other states and the District of Columbia, as well as the NFL. Kalshi's response to the petition is due November 9, and the Supreme Court is not expected to decide whether to take up the case until December at the earliest, ESPN reported.

#5065. U.S.-Sanctioned Moscow Exchange Will List Bitcoin on Dec. 1 as Russia Pulls Crypto Trading Home

Russia's largest exchange, Moscow Exchange,. will open bitcoin trading on Dec. 1 under the country's new crypto law, with retail buyers tested and capped at 300,000 rubles a year.

Moscow Exchange, Russia’s largest trading venue, will begin trading bitcoin, ether and Tether’s USDT on Dec. 1, the exchange announced this week, calling it the country’s first organized market for digital currencies.

The launch puts bitcoin on the same screens Russians use to trade Sberbank and Gazprom shares. It also puts a major crypto venue inside an institution that sits under U.S. sanctions. The first phase will run as a pilot. Participants will connect as they are ready, and the exchange said interest has come from existing members, their clients and businesses that already use digital currencies for investment and settlement.

Trading will run on the exchange’s existing stock market technology and follow its stock market hours, from 6:50 a.m. to 11:50 p.m. Moscow time, the exchange told Russian newspaper Vedomosti. The National Clearing Centre, the exchange’s clearing arm, will clear every trade.

From day one, clients can settle trades the same day or the next day, and the exchange will offer swaps to help participants manage liquidity. BTC, ETH and USDT will trade in both addressed and addressless modes, and the list is set to grow over time with Russian and international instruments.

Custody will run through partner digital depositaries, a new type of entity created under the country’s rules. A Moscow Exchange executive first previewed the plan on Oct. 31.

Retail access is tight. Non-qualified investors must pass a test before they can buy, and they can purchase no more than 300,000 rubles of digital currency a year, roughly $3,700, through a single broker. Qualified investors face no cap. The exchange had first flagged plans to debut bitcoin trading in October.

For the exchange, the structure keeps crypto inside a regime it already runs. Brokers connect the way they do for equities, trades net through the same clearing system and investor classifications carry over from the stock market. That lowers the barrier for banks and brokerages that want to offer bitcoin without building separate custody and matching systems of their own.

The U.S. Treasury blocked Moscow Exchange and the National Clearing Centre in June 2024 as part of a broader push against Russia’s financial system over the war in Ukraine. That makes the clearing house holding customers’ USDT a sanctioned entity.

Tether routinely freezes wallets tied to addresses on the Treasury’s sanctions list, which raises questions about how the stablecoin issuer would treat USDT held through the Moscow clearing structure. Neither the exchange nor Tether has publicly addressed that issue.

The choice of USDT, a dollar-pegged token, is notable for a market built in part to operate outside the dollar system. Bitcoin and ether carry no issuer that can freeze balances.

The market rests on Federal Law No. 282-FZ, “On Digital Currencies and Digital Rights,” whose main provisions took effect on Sept. 1. The law still bans using crypto for domestic payments, though exporters and importers can use it to settle cross-border trade.

Russian officials have spent the past year building the plumbing. Lawmakers handed securities regulators oversight of crypto last month, and the central bank licensed Sberbank for crypto work weeks earlier.

The goal is to move trading that Russians already do on offshore exchanges and peer-to-peer desks onto domestic, supervised rails where the state can see it and tax it. Moscow is betting that a familiar exchange, tied to brokers that Russian investors already use, can pull that volume back.

The open question is whether the caps and testing keep retail traders offshore, and whether foreign liquidity will touch a sanctioned venue at all. For now, Russia’s first organized bitcoin market opens on Dec. 1, with a pilot and a modest ceiling.

#5061. Canadian Man Extradited to US Over Plot to Hijack University Supercomputers for Crypto

Canadian Man Extradited to US Over Plot to Hijack University Supercomputers for Crypto

A Canadian man has been extradited to the United States to face charges that he helped hack into university computers to steal login credentials and secretly mine cryptocurrency, the Justice Department said on Tuesday.

A federal grand jury in the Western District of New York indicted Ryan James Roach, 35, of Saskatchewan, and Mathiew James Stubbings, 41, of Ontario, on charges of conspiracy to obtain information from and damage a protected computer, obtaining information from a protected computer, and intentionally damaging a protected computer. The charges carry a maximum sentence of 10 years in prison and a $250,000 fine.

Canadian man extradited to the U.S. for his role in computer hacking scheme targeting universities... @FBIBuffalo @USDOJ_Intl https://t.co/vY8IacXdZZ pic.twitter.com/NsSZ5BIkIf

Between June 2016 and December 2019, the pair targeted U.S. educational institutions with supercomputers, researching security weaknesses in their networks before breaking in, according to the indictment. Once inside, they allegedly deployed malware, stole login credentials and forced the victims' computers to mine cryptocurrency for their own benefit.

Victims included institutions in New York, Tennessee, North Carolina and Massachusetts, among them a university in the Western District of New York, prosecutors said. The pair allegedly used virtual private servers in several countries, registered under false names, to send malware and mining software to victim computers and store stolen credentials, and routed attacks through computers compromised at other victims to hide their identities.

Roach was extradited from Canada on October 28 and arraigned in Rochester, New York, where he was held pending a detention hearing. Extradition proceedings against Stubbings are continuing in Canada.

The scheme is a form of cryptojacking, in which attackers use other people's computers to mine crypto while the victims bear the electricity costs and slower performance. The Justice Department did not say which cryptocurrency the pair allegedly mined.

But hackers looking to profit from stolen computing power may be turning elsewhere. In a report published Monday, Google's Threat Intelligence Group said it expects attackers to "increasingly prioritize LLMJacking over traditional resource theft like cryptojacking," hijacking companies' access to AI models and passing on the bills.

Unlike the gradual drain of cryptojacking, LLMjacking can run up huge cloud charges within hours, Google said. In incidents investigated by its Mandiant unit this year, fraudulent charges topped $100,000 in a single day, and some organizations racked up $1 million before access was cut off. Attackers are reselling stolen access through underground services that Google calls "LLM Mixers."

#5058. Keel (KEEL) Sells Entire Bitcoin Treasury for $192 Million, Will Sell BTC Daily

Keel sold 2,520 bitcoin in the third quarter, left its treasury near zero and said Quebec-mined coins will now be sold every day as it pivots to AI data centers.

Keel Infrastructure sold its entire bitcoin treasury in the third quarter, offloading 2,520 bitcoin for $192 million and leaving the company with only nominal holdings at Sept. 30, the former Bitfarms miner reported Thursday.

The sales covered the 2,261 bitcoin Keel held going into the quarter plus the 259 it mined, and the company said it timed them during periods of strength. Going forward, bitcoin mined from Keel’s remaining Quebec operations will be sold on a daily basis, “effectively eliminating the Company’s exposure to Bitcoin price movements on treasury holdings.”

The move completes a shift Keel began earlier this year, when the company, then still trading as Bitfarms (BITF), started selling its stack and winding down U.S. mining to fund high-performance computing and AI infrastructure.

Keel Infrastructure Corp. rebranded from Bitfarms after completing a U.S. redomiciliation to Delaware in April and now describes itself as a North American digital infrastructure and energy company. Legacy bitcoin mining still shows up in the results, but the center of gravity has moved.

Revenue from continuing operations fell to $28 million from $68 million a year earlier, largely because Keel stopped U.S. bitcoin mining in the second quarter and because the average bitcoin price declined. Adjusted EBITDA swung to a negative $27 million from a positive $19 million. General and administrative expenses roughly doubled to $34 million as the company hired senior specialists and booked higher stock-based compensation. The company still posted about $780 million of unrestricted cash as of Nov. 4 and said it has no current need for debt or equity financing before signing a lease.

“Power supply is getting scarcer while demand keeps climbing,” CEO Ben Gagnon said in the release. “Keel’s 2027 and 2028 power is further advanced, more secure, and closer to delivery than it was last quarter.”

CFO Jonathan Mir added that capital deployed ahead of a lease becomes equity in project financing and that the company examines opportunities to recycle capital on an ongoing basis.

Construction began at Moses Lake in September after Keel cleared Notice to Proceed, and the company expects it to be the first energized site generating HPC revenue in 2027. It also engineered an optional plan for next-generation Vera Rubin architecture at the site. Permitting advanced at Sharon and Panther Creek in Pennsylvania, and load studies could expand secured data center capacity from 478 MW toward nearly 1.9 GW if Panther Creek and Scrubgrass expansions proceed. Keel has also moved to consolidate older bitcoin sites into a single AI campus.

The development pipeline stands at about 2.2 gigawatts with grid interconnections already in place across Pennsylvania, Washington State and Québec. That is the inventory Keel is trying to lease before the cash from the bitcoin sale and the remaining Quebec mining runs down.

Keel is not alone. Riot Platforms and Hut 8 have sold thousands of bitcoin this year to fund AI builds and share buybacks, and Riot this week named Anthropic as the tenant on a roughly $28 billion Texas lease. Public miners that once marketed themselves as bitcoin accumulators are turning their power and land into AI capacity instead.

Keel’s remaining mining footprint sits in Quebec, where regulated power rates still support production. Those coins will no longer sit on the balance sheet. The company will sell them the day they are mined, converting hashrate into cash for the infrastructure build rather than into a treasury that moves with the bitcoin price.

The open question is whether the leases close on the terms Gagnon described before the cash pile is spent. Keel says competitive tension is high at all three near-term sites and that multiple creditworthy parties are at the table. Until a signed lease shows up, the $192 million bitcoin sale is the clearest proof that the company has left the treasury strategy behind.

#5056. French Hill’s race still too close to call as House flips, changing the direction of crypto legislation

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Rep. Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill.

As of Wednesday afternoon, House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill's race for reelection is too close to call as a Democratic takeover in the House is set to usher in a new slate of leaders and reshape the future of crypto legislation in Congress.

On Tuesday night into Wednesday, Hill has been neck and neck with Democratic candidate Chris Jones in Arkansas, according to the Associated Press, with 99% of votes already reported. Hill held a slim lead at publication time.

Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill. The latter stalled in the Senate in September. Crypto political action committee Fairshake and other PACs spent $1 million on Hill's campaign, according to reporting from the Arkansas Democrat Gazette.

Other crypto-friendly lawmakers also secured reelection, including Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., Rep. Warren Davidson, R-Ohio, Rep. Ritchie Torres, D-N.Y., and current House Agriculture Committee Chair Glenn "GT" Thompson, R-Pa. Later in the day on Wednesday, Rep. Bill Huizenga, R-MI., who is viewed favorably by the crypto industry, lost his seat to Democratic candidate Sean McCann, according to the Associated Press.

Candidate Shomari Figures beat Republican candidate Rhett Marques in Alabama. Figures was endorsed by crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, given his vote on sweeping digital asset legislation called the Clarity Act.

With the House flipping control, Democrats will usher in the agenda for key congressional committees that have jurisdiction over crypto and prediction markets. Top Democrat Maxine Waters will likely take back her spot as House Financial Services Committee chair, leaving Hill out of his top spot, but likely to be the top Republican on that committee.

Waters is still expected to take up the spot again as chair, but has since been challenged by Democratic Reps. Sean Casten and Bill Foster. Some Democrats have signaled that they want "generational change," given that Waters is 88 years old and Casten is 54, according to reporting from Politico last month.

Regarding crypto, Waters worked on the stablecoin framework dubbed the GENIUS Act and has shown an openness to writing rules for the industry. However, she has also criticized the industry following the collapse of crypto exchange FTX and, later, President Donald Trump's crypto interests and how that can influence regulation.

#5054. Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance has removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus, a third-party crypto trading platform, after SignalPlus reported a "potential security issue," the exchange said on Thursday.

"Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe," the exchange said, describing the move as an "immediate precautionary measure."

Following a potential security issue reported by third-party platform SignalPlus, we have proactively removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus as an immediate precautionary measure.

Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe.

If you used SignalPlus, please… pic.twitter.com/0HYYkTAW2p

API keys let third-party apps and trading tools connect to a user's exchange account and place trades on their behalf. Removing them cuts off that connection, meaning any automated strategies running on Binance through SignalPlus will stop until users set up new keys.

Binance urged SignalPlus users to log into their accounts directly to review their positions and open orders. It recommended that they wait until SignalPlus confirms the issue is fully resolved before reconnecting, and that they always enable IP whitelisting, which restricts an API key to approved internet addresses.

The exchange also called on all third-party platforms integrated with Binance to review their security controls, watch for unusual activity and contact it immediately if they spot any concerns.

SignalPlus, a Hong Kong-based platform that provides trading tools for crypto options and derivatives, connects to several exchanges, including Binance.

The incident comes a week after Binance was hit by the largest hack in crypto history, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from its hot wallets on October 29. The FBI has attributed the theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor, a claim Pyongyang has denied.

Binance has said the attack began when a fake recruiter persuaded one of its engineers to download a malware-laced coding assignment, and that the attackers then used stolen credentials to reach an internal wallet signing service.

Binance said on Wednesday that it has replenished about 98% of the stolen assets from its own funds, and is still buying the rest, mostly less liquid tokens, through market and over-the-counter channels. It has also taken a new proof-of-reserves snapshot covering 44 tokens, up from 10, which users will be able to verify once its zero-knowledge proof is generated in about 50 to 60 hours.

The exchange is still restoring services. On Thursday, it began reopening withdrawals for more assets in phases, prioritizing tokens based on user demand over the past week. Deposits remain open.

#5052. South Korea Pushes 22% Crypto Tax to 2029 in Fourth Delay, Offers Three-Year Loss Carryforward

South Korea's government will ask lawmakers to delay its 22% crypto gains tax to 2029 and let investors carry losses forward for up to three years.

South Korea’s government plans to push back its tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029, Finance and Economy Minister Lee Hyeong-il said Thursday, in what would be the fourth delay since the levy was written into law.

The tax is scheduled to take effect in January. To postpone it, the National Assembly must amend the Income Tax Act before then, and Lee said the government would seek lawmakers’ approval during this year’s regular session.

“It weighs heavily on me to propose another delay after the tax has already been postponed three times,” Lee said at a press briefing in Seoul.

Korea legislated the tax in 2020 for a 2022 start. It was then pushed to 2023, 2025 and 2027 as officials conceded that tax systems and investor protections were not ready.

The main reason for the new delay is data on overseas trading. When investors move tokens bought abroad onto a Korean exchange, the exchange may have no record of the purchase price needed to calculate a taxable gain.

That gap closes in 2029, when Korea and the United States begin exchanging information under the OECD-backed Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework, or CARF. Introducing the tax before then could put Korea’s domestic crypto market at a disadvantage, the government said.

“Once we begin receiving cryptocurrency transaction data from the United States in 2029, we will have enough information to tax investors who use US exchanges on the same basis as those who trade in the domestic market,” Lee said.

The government also cited the need to align the tax with the proposed Digital Asset Act, which would set rules for digital asset businesses and strengthen investor protection. Korea’s broader crypto rulebook has been moving on several fronts, including a Bank of Korea push to write stablecoins into foreign exchange law.

Exchanges, investors and lawmakers had pressed for more time, with exchanges saying unclear reporting requirements held up system development and testing.

The rate itself is not changing. Individuals would pay 22%, including local income tax, on annual net income above 2.5 million won, or about $1,869, from selling or lending virtual assets such as bitcoin.

What is new is a proposed three-year loss carryforward. Under current law, only losses from the same year can offset gains, so an investor could owe tax in a profitable year even after losing more the year before.

Under the proposal, losses realized in 2029 could offset gains through 2032. Losses realized before the tax takes effect would not be eligible, the government said, citing the volatility of crypto prices as the reason for the change.

The carryforward would follow similar practices in the U.S. and Britain, the finance ministry said. It also said it will work with the Financial Services Commission to speed up the digital asset law.

The government plans to use the extra two years to collect overseas trading records and analyze them alongside domestic tax data. A working group of industry representatives, experts and government officials will review tax rules for different types of transactions and ways to simplify filing through the first half of 2027.

If the review finds that more legislation is needed, the government plans to submit amendments during the National Assembly’s 2027 regular session. Detailed tax guidelines are due in the first half of 2028.

For Korea’s large base of retail crypto traders, the announcement extends a tax-free window that was supposed to close in 2022. Whether 2029 holds depends on the Assembly passing the amendment in the coming weeks and on the U.S. data exchange starting on schedule.

#5050. Bitcoin rebounds past $80,000 as markets shrug off midterms and Binance hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Ether jumped nearly 6% and crypto stocks rallied, while Bitwise says the market’s muted reaction to the Binance hack shows growing maturity.

Bitcoin has recovered from its post-Binance hack losses, surging toward $81,000 following Tuesday's U.S. midterm elections, which saw Democrats regain control of both chambers of Congress.

"Markets took the Democratic win in stride," Nexo analysts wrote in their daily dispatch. "Futures fell as much as 2.5% on early counts and then recovered fully, which suggests investors saw the outcome as adding clarity and not as changing the growth story."

Bitcoin (BTC) was up 3.5% to $80,500 on Wednesday afternoon, while Ethereum jumped nearly 6% to $2,433. Solana and XRP were up a more muted 2.5% on the day.

Futures data suggests traders are adjusting existing positions rather than taking on additional leverage, according to Nexo. Crypto futures volume climbed 27%, while open interest rose just 3%, and Bitcoin open interest grew more slowly than its price. Roughly $469 million in leveraged positions were also flushed out.

Nexo said market orders have shifted to a slight net buying bias after favoring sellers the previous day, while the most actively traded Bitcoin options are predominantly calls.

Crypto-related stocks also rallied, with Strategy and Coinbase gaining about 6% each; Strategy's variable-rate perpetual preferred stock STRC hit $100 for the first time since May.

"The case for holding Bitcoin crosses party lines," Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said earlier Wednesday.

Block Inc. jumped 15% following its earnings report, while bitcoin treasury firm Strive climbed roughly 12%. Meanwhile, the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin fell 17%.

Bitwise CIO Matt Hougan argued that the market's relatively muted reaction to the Binance hack reflects the crypto industry's growing maturity and improved security infrastructure.

In his weekly blog memo, Hougan examined the 10 largest crypto-related hacks in history and found that Bitcoin finished higher in the following 30 days in five cases and lower in five, suggesting no consistent relationship between major security incidents and Bitcoin's subsequent performance.

He said Bitcoin's roughly 4% decline following the Binance breach was largely in line with broader risk assets, emphasizing that Binance was hacked, not the Bitcoin network.

"Hacks would impact the market if they suggested there is no safer way to hold bitcoin, but there is. In this case, the stolen crypto was held in online hot wallets at a large, integrated crypto exchange," Hougan wrote.

He pointed to institutional custodians, cold storage and spot Bitcoin ETFs as increasingly secure alternatives for investors, highlighting ETFs in particular because their underlying assets are held in offline cold storage and subject to multiple withdrawal approvals and restrictions.

Hougan's assessment was echoed by Matt Mena, crypto research strategist at 21Shares, who said the market's resilience following the Binance breach was encouraging.

"What we find most encouraging is how the market handled the worst of it," Mena wrote in a Tuesday report. "The largest hack in crypto’s history caused a far smaller drawdown than past crises; the protocols kept running, and none of the structural drivers broke: banks, payment networks, and asset managers kept shipping onchain products through the drawdown, and regulators kept writing rules."

Mena is watching four things into November: whether Bitcoin holds $76,000 to $78,000 and sustains its move back above $80,000 to $82,000; Binance's progress in completing withdrawals and proving its reserves; credit spreads, oil prices, and long-end Treasury yields; and whether ETF inflows return.

#5048. Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Samsung is putting digital dollars in the same app where its users keep their credit cards and boarding passes.

Stablecoin accounts went live in Samsung Wallet on Thursday for eligible U.S. Galaxy users, letting them buy, hold, send, and receive Circle’s USDC on the Solana blockchain without downloading a separate crypto app or managing private keys. The feature reaches about 82 million compatible Galaxy devices in the U.S., according to the announcement.

“For a decade, stablecoins have proved the technology works,” said Lily Liu, president of the Solana Foundation. “What they lacked was a way to reach the mainstream.”

Samsung first announced the feature on Oct. 7, when the electronics giant tapped Solana for USDC transfers. Samsung Wallet first teased plans to support the stablecoin, which is pegged to the U.S. dollar, over the summer.

The product is built with Bastion, a licensed stablecoin custodian and infrastructure provider, and Coinbase, which safeguards the USDC as Bastion’s sub-custodian through its Coinbase Prime Vault. Samsung’s disclosures stress that the account is a crypto wallet, not a bank account.

Users can send USDC to other Samsung Wallet users in the U.S. and to compatible outside crypto wallets with no Samsung transfer fee, though other providers may charge one. They can also send money to qualifying bank accounts in more than 60 countries, where recipients may receive local currency, with fees that vary by destination.

Topping up with a credit card, including through Samsung Pay, carries a 4% fee, and withdrawals to a bank account or debit card also come with a fee. New users who make a first top-up of $50 or more can receive $25 in USDC. The service requires Android 13 or later and is open to U.S. residents 18 and older, except those in New York.

"Stablecoins have the potential to make moving money around the world faster and easier, and we want Galaxy users to be able to take advantage of that without having to navigate the complexity of traditional crypto tools,” said Woncheol Chai, who leads the digital wallet team in Samsung’s mobile business. “Solana helps us bring that experience to Samsung's scale."

Samsung said it expects to explore online and in-store stablecoin payments and to expand to additional markets, subject to local regulation.

#5046. Bull Bitcoin Becomes First Exchange to Add Payjoin Privacy Deposits, Withdrawals, and Payjoin-by-Default Trading

Bull Bitcoin announced deep Payjoin integration with its exchange, claiming to be the first Bitcoin exchange in the world to offer Payjoin as a deposit and withdrawal option. The update unlocks two new concepts: “Payjoin-by-default and Payjoin trading,” according to he press release shared with Bitcoin Magazine.

Founded by Francis Pouliot in Canada in 2013, Bull Bitcoin is the world’s longest-running Bitcoin-only exchange. Its non-custodial platform lets customers in Canada, Europe, and Latin America buy Bitcoin directly into their own wallet and sell Bitcoin to their bank account. Bull Bitcoin also develops BULL Wallet, a free and open-source self-custodial Bitcoin wallet. The company has been self-funded since its inception.

Every time a BULL Wallet user buys or sells Bitcoin on-chain via their built-in exchange, that transaction will be a Payjoin by default. This will allow Bitcoin buyers to passively aggregate their wallet’s UTXOs, which can reduce their Bitcoin mining fees in the future. “Many Bitcoin users do not even know that they must periodically “merge” their coins by spending them to a new address in their Bitcoin wallet, if they want to reduce the economic costs of future Bitcoin transactions,” explained the press release. Payjoin-by-default automates this process. Not only does this help reduce the size of Bitcoin’s UTXO set, making it easier for everyone to run a Bitcoin node, but it also reduces fee pressure for every Bitcoin user.

Payjoin trading is a novel concept that protects users from malicious actors trying to gain insights into their financial habits by conducting chain analysis. Because transaction amounts in Bitcoin are public, it’s possible to reverse-engineer information such as someone’s salary or trading strategies such as dollar-cost averaging. Bull Bitcoin is a non-custodial exchange, so every trade leaves an on-chain trail.

In December 2024, BULL Wallet became the world’s first Bitcoin wallet to integrate the Payjoin V2 protocol. This was a highly experimental project coordinated with Dan Gould and the Payjoin Dev Kit team, who have since founded the Payjoin Foundation.

Researchers have used the insights from this experimentation to improve the Payjoin experience to make it more seamless and resistant to attacks. The software powering Payjoin, both for the wallet and for the exchange, is fully free and open-source.

To benefit from these features, all a user has to do is download BULL Wallet, create a Bull Bitcoin exchange account, and log into their account via the BULL mobile app; “The rest happens automatically, like magic!”

#5044. Democratic sweep of Congress forces crypto industry to rethink Washington strategy

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

As results rolled in, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate.

After two years of a pro-crypto, pro-innovation climate in Washington, the digital asset industry faces a sharp political jolt as Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress, forcing the sector to seek new allies within a party that has been more divided on crypto.

As results rolled in on Tuesday, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate. Eight anti-crypto Senators were also elected alongside 106 in the House.

Following the results, some in the crypto industry have taken the position that crypto is bipartisan and that lawmakers on both sides of the aisle are pro-digital assets. But a Democratic sweep means more oversight, and there is close to no chance of the crypto industry getting legislation it wants on the table.

"Nobody has their head in the sand," said Kara Calvert, vice president of U.S. policy at Coinbase, in a post on X. "There will be headwinds for any bipartisan legislation next year, but crypto is 100% bipartisan."

Many of the new members in Congress are pro-crypto, said Kevin Wysocki, head of policy at Anchorage Digital.

"The younger members are more used to this technology, are more open to new technology that maybe rivals some of the existing technology," he said in an interview with The Block on Wednesday. "It's very bullish, I think, for crypto overall, so I think that's one of the great things from what happened yesterday, and I think it's only going to get better over time, regardless of who's in power, Republicans or Democrats."

However, one of the biggest races the crypto industry had its eyes on, and money on, was the Ohio Senate race between former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown and SWC-endorsed Republican Sen. Jon Husted. The crypto political action committee spent tens of millions of dollars on that race against Brown, but ultimately lost when Brown defeated Husted later in the night.

Brown was previously Senate Banking Committee Chair, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and has been viewed as critical of crypto. He could have a leading role in oversight and regulation of the industry. And since he won in a special election to fill Vice President JD Vance's Senate term, that means Brown could return to the Senate as early as Dec. 15.

Brown's return is significant, Wysocki said. When he was chair of the Senate Banking Committee, Brown and his Senate staff were open to constructive conversations about crypto, he said.

Pro-crypto political action committees reportedly spent over $60 million alone in the Senate race in Ohio against Brown. On its own, crypto PAC Fairshake raised $106.2 million, according to Americans for Financial Reform. And crypto PACs overall spent $170 million on the midterms, according to Fortune, with millions going to Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts in Nebraska.

Fairshake spent $154.5 million in both primaries and the general election, with over $13.5 million going to Ricketts.

"Fairshake played a meaningful role in winning competitive races across the country in 2026 and we will do it again in 2028," said Geoff Vetter, spokesperson for Fairshake, in an emailed statement to The Block. "Newly elected and current members will have a choice: protect consumers and American jobs or fight progress. We will be watching and '28 is a new map."

One crypto industry source alluded that PACs and trade associations will have to "recalibrate" and need to "double down on being bipartisan."

Specifically on Fairshake, the person criticized funds being used on Brown's race.

"What did we ultimately get out of that?" they said. "Where could that money have been better spent? To be perfectly honest with you, I think it could have been better spent on shoe-leather lobbying."

That money could have been spent to lobby lawmakers on the Clarity Act, broad crypto legislation that passed the House, but got stuck in the Senate in September, they said.

"You spend a fraction of it, and you could have effectively hired all of DC to lobby on Clarity," they added.

Focus will turn to taxes and security and hacks, Anchorage's Wysocki said. The election, too, comes just days after the largest crypto hack on record of Binance for over $6 billion.

"Congress is going to be asking those questions, like how do we make security at our financial institutions stronger?" Wysocki said.

The Clarity Act that passed out of the House last year and stalled in the Senate a few months ago is unlikely to advance over the next two years given Brown's win, said investment bank TD Cowen.

Oversight will also come into view, including investigations into Trump-family-run World Liberty Financial, said TD Cowen’s Washington Research Group, led by managing director Jaret Seiberg.

"Democrats will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements, including World Liberty Financial," Seiberg wrote in a note on Wednesday. "We believe the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

#5042. Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🗳️ Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Market Structure Bill Resurfaces

Bitcoin spent Tuesday night under $76,000, a seven-week low, with traders bracing for whatever a Democratic Congress meant for crypto. By Wednesday afternoon it was back above $80,800, up 4% on the day.

The easy story is that uncertainty lifted and crypto exhaled. And that could be part of it. But oil helped. WTI fell 9% to $88 a barrel, down almost 17% on the week, as Iran’s parliament speaker said voters had “said NO TO WAR.” The DOW jumped 3% on the day and Nasdaq +1.6% in what was a huge green day across markets.

James Butterfill of CoinShares put it plainly: “For Bitcoin, real yields, dollar liquidity and ETP flows still carry more weight than the elections.”

But the policy news that landed Wednesday afternoon matters more than the candles.

Eleanor Terrett reported that House Democrats are discussing expanding Maxine Waters’ Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, originally an amendment to the CLARITY Act in 2025, into a full market structure bill next Congress. The draft would fold in Democratic CLARITY provisions that the House rejected last year, plus provisions Senate Democrats and Republicans had already agreed on before the September cloture vote that killed the bill. Three industry sources described the talks to her.

That’s a genuine surprise. Twenty-four hours ago the consensus was that market structure legislation was dead for two years. Waters is the likely incoming chair of House Financial Services, and she is apparently planning to write a bill rather than hold hearings. But the bill will have some changes.

The new proposals under discussion would give the SEC a larger role supervising crypto markets and leave the CFTC with a smaller one. The draft is expected to carry ethics provisions restricting the crypto activities of Trump and other federal officials. Those provisions are what Democrats wanted all along, and what many thought would kill any chances the bill has of getting signed.

So one of the first courses of action from this Democrat-led Congress is to enact some reasonable changes to the Clarity Act to try to get it passed in 2027. That feels like a very bullish first step.

#5041. Greece Approves 10% Crypto Capital Gains Tax as First Framework Lands Below Draft Rate

Greece's parliament approved a 10% capital gains tax on cryptocurrencies, the country's first dedicated crypto tax framework, with annual gains up to €500 exempt.

Greece’s parliament approved on Thursday a bill that imposes a 10% capital gains tax on cryptocurrencies, the first comprehensive legal framework for taxing crypto assets in the country.

Cryptocurrency capital gains of up to €500 ($563) a year will be exempt from the new tax, the bill says. For bitcoin holders in Greece, that threshold is a small buffer, not a carve-out for larger positions.

“Further implementation directives will be published in the coming weeks, which will also define transparency and audit rules,” a senior finance ministry official told Reuters.

An earlier draft circulated in October had floated a 15% rate. The version lawmakers passed lands five points lower, while keeping the €500 annual exemption that was already in that proposal.

The tax is set to apply from Jan. 1, 2027, Greek coverage of the finance ministry bill says. It is levied on the net gain — the difference between the acquisition price and the transfer price — not on the full sale proceeds. Trading fees and other costs tied to the deal can factor into that calculation.

A taxable transfer is not limited to selling for euros. Using cryptocurrency to buy goods or services, or contributing it to cover or increase company capital, can also crystallize a gain under the new rules. That matters for anyone in Greece who pays a merchant in bitcoin rather than converting first.

Simply holding bitcoin while its euro value rises does not, by itself, create a tax bill. Crypto-to-crypto swaps, such as bitcoin for another token, are treated as non-taxable events in the explanatory material that accompanied the bill, matching the approach France has used to avoid taxing every hop between coins.

Net annual gains at or below €500 are exempt. Greek analyses of the text say that once net gains clear that ceiling, the 10% rate applies to the full net gain for the year, not only the slice above €500. Losses above €500 in a tax year may be carried forward for up to five years and offset only against future crypto capital gains.

Purchases of crypto assets are also being pulled into Greece’s existing “source of funds” checks from 2027, so buyers may need to show that the euros used to acquire bitcoin or other tokens came from declared income or other lawful capital. The rule does not add a separate purchase tax; it widens the audit trail around how coins were funded.

European Union countries do not share a unified tax system for the sector. Rates on cryptocurrency gains typically run from about 8% to 30% across member states and are usually levied on capital gains, the finance ministry official said. Greece’s 10% sits toward the softer end of that band, below France’s often-cited 30% treatment and above Cyprus’s 8%.

The same patchwork is shifting elsewhere in the bloc. The Netherlands has moved toward a realized capital gains tax from 2028, while Germany and Portugal still exempt crypto held longer than a year. Since January, the EU’s DAC8 reporting rules have required crypto platforms to send user and transaction data to national tax authorities — a tool Greece already wrote into domestic law.

State revenue from the new levy could not be projected, the official said, because the vast majority of investors use platforms abroad, leaving the size of Greece’s cryptocurrency market hard to measure.

For bitcoin holders who trade on foreign exchanges, that gap is the practical test. The rate is now set in law; whether Athens can match 10% on paper with enforceable cost-basis data from offshore platforms is the question the coming directives will have to answer.

#5039. Crédit Mutuel Arkéa confirms cyberattack after ZachXBT flags $133M in suspected losses

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa did not confirm the exact amount of losses but validated the order of magnitude of the $133 million figure flagged by ZachXBT.

French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has confirmed that a cyberattack enabled fraudulent transfers from its account, which were converted into crypto via specialized platforms.

In a statement released Wednesday, the bank said that client assets were unaffected and the incident has been contained.

"As soon as this attack was identified, our teams immediately activated the necessary security measures to contain the incident and prevent any further fraudulent movements against Crédit Mutuel Arkéa," the bank said.

"We wish to reassure all of our customers: this incident has had no impact on their assets," the bank added. "It only caused a delay in the execution of certain transfers."

Onchain sleuth ZachXBT, who first publicly flagged the exploit on Nov. 2, estimated the losses at more than $133 million. He noted that the figure could rise, as the attack unfolded over several days and may have involved additional over-the-counter transactions.

While Crédit Mutuel Arkéa did not confirm the exact amount of losses, it validated the order of magnitude. The bank said some of the stolen funds had been blocked.

"While it is therefore still too early to communicate a final amount, we can already state that the amounts involved are in no way of a nature to undermine the financial strength and soundness of Crédit Mutuel Arkéa," the bank said.

ZachXBT previously reported that the attacker had been moving stolen funds through a series of transactions, including swapping USDC for ETH, routing assets through instant exchanges such as WellWallet and Kyrrex, converting funds into XMR via Wagyu, and bridging assets across multiple chains.

He also said he has a "high degree of confidence" that the attacker was not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

The bank has over 12,000 employees and more than 5.8 million members and customers. Its reported total assets are €211.5 billion ($238.1 billion), according to the statement.

#5037. Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman has hit back at criticism of his comments about computer memory, saying they were taken out of context and that he has long argued for the world to make more of it.

"I actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context," Altman tweeted early Wednesday. "I have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time."

but only because...i actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context.

i have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time.

The post came as Altman leaned into the backlash. Replying to users joking about his remarks, he said he "lol'd" at one meme and, when another asked him to rename his X account "Ram Altman," replied "ok yeah," briefly changing his display name before explaining he had only done so because he wanted people to have plenty of memory.

Altman's original comments came in an interview with YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, where he discussed shortages of memory and other components caused by the AI boom. "From like a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time," he said. "You want maximum utilization in a cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment."

In the same interview, Altman also blamed memory makers for being slow to expand production, and said he would be "very sad" if prices for chips and other components did not fall.

The remarks drew widespread criticism, including from Elon Musk, who mocked Altman on Sunday with a meme of the Inglourious Basterds character Hans Landa asking, "You are withholding DRAM from the cloud, are you not?" "The people deserve to have their RAM too!" Musk wrote.

The spat comes amid a global memory shortage driven by demand from AI data centers. SK hynix, one of the world's largest memory makers, warned last week that demand for memory would outstrip supply through 2030.

Contract prices for 16GB DDR5 memory chips rose from $8.70 in October 2025 to $48 in September 2026, an increase of more than 550%, according to Tom's Hardware. On an earnings call this week, Chen Ching-wen, CEO of memory maker Team Group, said there was "no downward price trend whatsoever," and warned that some buyers could face a situation where "even money can't buy goods."

The crunch has already pushed up prices for consumer devices. In April, Meta raised the price of its Quest 3 and Quest 3S headsets by $50 to $100, weeks after Sony increased PlayStation 5 prices.

#5034. The Digital Chamber calls Sherrod Brown’s win ‘most consequential unfavorable result’ for crypto

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Digital Chamber said Sherrod Brown’s priorities in financial stability, consumer protection, banking oversight, and illicit-finance concerns could affect the party's overall approach to crypto.

The Digital Chamber called Sherrod Brown's win the "most consequential unfavorable result for crypto," and said his priorities in financial stability, consumer protection, banking oversight, and illicit-finance concerns could affect the Democratic Party's overall approach to crypto.

"His influence could extend beyond committee votes to the Democratic caucus's overall approach to crypto," the industry group said. "Engagement should be substantive and focused on consumer protections and accountability."

Brown, a longtime crypto skeptic, won the U.S. Senate special election in Ohio on Nov. 3, defeating Republican Jon Husted. Crypto political action committees (PACs) spent more than $63 million supporting Husted against Brown, according to The Digital Chamber.

Outside of Brown's win, Democrats are set to control both chambers of Congress after a larger-than-expected victory on Nov. 3. The Senate stood at 54 Democrats to 44 Republicans with two races uncalled, while Democrats held 240 House seats with 48 races yet to be called, according to AP.

The Digital Chamber stated that the results shift the policy debate from whether Washington should embrace technological innovation to what protections, accountability, and economic benefits should accompany it.

Furthermore, Democratic committee chairs will hold subpoena power and can investigate regulators, cryptocurrency exchanges, AI developers, and data-center development. Digital asset legislation will depend more heavily on Democratic priorities including consumer protection, financial stability, illicit finance, and market integrity, the group said.

On Senate Banking, the Digital Chamber identified Elizabeth Warren as the clear favorite to become chair. It described the committee as the largest potential obstacle to the Clarity Act and other market-structure legal frameworks for the crypto industry. Clarity failed to pass a procedural vote in the Senate in September.

"A Democratic-controlled Congress does not necessarily mean comprehensive crypto legislation is permanently dead," the group said. "It does, however, mean the industry cannot simply continue the same legislative strategy that neglects working with moderate Democrats."

The group said that there are still viable legislative openings around issues including: tax reporting, custody, stablecoin implementation, and consumer asset segregation.

Meanwhile, the Digital Chamber also highlighted that among other Democratic winners, James Talarico of Texas is supportive of clear cryptocurrency rules while advancing a strong AI-accountability agenda that includes an AI dividend and requirements that data centers cover infrastructure costs.

Josh Turek, who won a Senate seat in Iowa, is supportive of market-structure legislation, self-custody rights, and ending improper debanking, the group said. Mark Warner of Virginia, who won reelection, is also identified as an existing Democratic bridge on digital asset issues because of his seats on Banking and the Digital Assets Subcommittee.

"This is an important reminder that a Democratic congressional victory does not automatically translate into opposition to crypto," the group said.
